717d281da1fcb6f2	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	§ 43.3 — Persons authorized to perform maintenance, preventive maintenance, rebuilding, and alterations	Who is allowed to work on an aircraft. Mechanics, repairmen, repair stations and air carriers each get their own paragraph; paragraph (g) is the pilot&#x27;s: a private pilot (or higher, not sport) may perform preventive maintenance on an aircraft they own or operate, as long as it is not flown under Part 121, 129 or 135. Paragraph (k) says avionics database updates done from the flight deck without tools are not maintenance at all.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::43.3
3b8fa2b465a2581a	Basic	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	Can I change the oil and replace a landing light bulb on the airplane I rent?	Only if you own or operate it; § 43.3(g) lets a private pilot do preventive maintenance on an aircraft owned or operated by that pilot, and the item must be on the Part 43 Appendix A list. Then make the required maintenance record entry.<br><br>§ 43.3 — Persons authorized to perform maintenance, preventive maintenance, rebuilding, and alterations; § 91.405 — Maintenance required	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::43.3

927853579a13b74e	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	§ 61.15 — Offenses involving alcohol or drugs	Drug and alcohol offenses and your certificate. A drug conviction, or an act prohibited by § 91.17 or § 91.19, is grounds for denying a certificate for up to a year and for suspending or revoking one you hold. A second motor vehicle action (DUI conviction or license action) within 3 years is the same. Every motor vehicle action must be reported in writing to the FAA security division within 60 days.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::61.15

89febf73cf4b108d	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	§ 61.31 — Type rating requirements, additional training, and authorization requirements	When a rating alone is not enough. Large and turbojet aircraft need a type rating. Complex, high-performance (more than 200 horsepower), pressurized (service ceiling above 25,000 ft MSL) and tailwheel airplanes each need training and a one-time logbook endorsement before you act as PIC. Paragraph (d)(2) is how a student pilot flies solo: training plus a solo endorsement instead of a rating, and (m)(2)(ii) exempts student pilots from the rating limitations.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::61.31

380e03c8ec11342f	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	§ 61.51 — Pilot logbooks	What goes in your logbook and what you may log. You must record the training and experience used for a certificate, rating, flight review or recency, with date, time, route, aircraft, and conditions of flight. Solo time means you were the sole occupant. A private pilot logs PIC when sole manipulator of an aircraft they are rated in; a student logs PIC only when sole occupant with a solo endorsement and in training. You must show your certificate, medical and logbook on request, and a student must carry the logbook and student certificate on every solo cross-country.	far-aim ppl::pre-solo cite::61.51

3216b22205806844	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	§ 61.53 — Prohibition on operations during medical deficiency	Do not fly when you are not medically fit. A medical-certificate holder may not act as PIC or required crew while knowing of a condition that would fail the medical standards, or while taking medication or treatment that would. Under BasicMed or when no medical is required, the standard is knowing of any condition that would make you unable to operate safely.	far-aim ppl::pre-solo cite::61.53

d2765aa39435afab	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	§ 61.109 — Aeronautical experience	The hours for the airplane single-engine private certificate: at least 40 total, including 20 of flight training and 10 solo. The training must include 3 hours cross-country, 3 hours at night with a cross-country over 100 nm and 10 full-stop takeoffs and landings, 3 hours by reference to instruments, and 3 hours of checkride preparation within the preceding 2 calendar months. The solo time must include 5 hours cross-country, one 150 nm cross-country with full-stop landings at three points and one leg over 50 nm, and three full-stop takeoffs and landings at a towered airport. Simulators may credit 2.5 hours, and an approved Part 142 course needs only 35 hours.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::61.109

Simulators may credit 2.5 hours, and an approved Part 142 course needs only 35 hours.	“One solo cross country flight of 150 nautical miles total distance, with full-stop landings at three points, and one segment of the flight consisting of a straight-line distance of more than 50 nautical miles” (§ 61.109 — Aeronautical experience (a)(5)(ii))	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::61.109

Airport and Seaplane Base Operations	§ 91.123 — Compliance with ATC clearances and instructions	Once you have a clearance you may not deviate from it unless you get an amended clearance, an emergency exists, or you are responding to a TCAS resolution advisory. Never act on a clearance issued to another aircraft.	“shall submit a detailed report of that emergency within 48 hours to the manager of that ATC facility, if requested by ATC” (§ 91.123 — Compliance with ATC clearances and instructions (d))	far-aim ppl::pre-solo cite::91.123

Navigation	AIM 1-1-3 — VHF Omni-directional Range (VOR)	The VOR: 108.0 to 117.95 MHz, line-of-sight, course accuracy of about plus or minus 1 degree, identified only by its Morse code or recorded voice ident. 4969a2267a81daaf	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::VI. Navigation	AIM 1-1-17 — Global Positioning System (GPS)	GPS for pilots: the constellation is designed so at least five satellites are visible, four are needed for a 3D position, and RAIM needs five (or four plus baro-aiding) to detect a bad satellite. For VFR the AIM&#x27;s cautions are the point: VFR and hand-held receivers have no RAIM alerting, databases need not be current for VFR but should be, antenna placement matters, and you must not rely on GPS alone. VFR waypoints are five-letter names starting with VP, not for use with ATC and not allowed on IFR flight plans. The IFR half covers approved equipment, database and approach requirements.	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::1-1-17

VFR and hand-held units do not alert on its loss, so you must cross-check position with other navigation.<br><br>AIM 1-1-17 — Global Positioning System (GPS)	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::1-1-17

Airport and Seaplane Base Operations	AIM 4-1-15 — Radar Traffic Information Service	Radar traffic information service (&#x27;flight following&#x27;): the controller points out targets that may conflict, by clock position, distance, direction of travel and type and altitude if known. Airport and Seaplane Base Operations	AIM 4-1-18 — Terminal Radar Services for VFR Aircraft	Terminal radar services for VFR aircraft come in tiers: basic radar service (safety alerts, traffic advisories, limited vectoring and, at some airports, sequencing); TRSA service (separation and sequencing, voluntary, decline with &#x27;negative TRSA service&#x27;); Class C service (separation between IFR and VFR plus sequencing to the primary airport); and Class B service (separation of all aircraft). f07fdeafa12fcb41	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::II. Preflight Procedures	AIM 4-3-18 — Taxiing	Taxiing at a towered airport: get approval before moving onto the movement area, state your position when you call, and get a clearance before taxiing on a runway, taking off or landing. Every runway crossing needs an explicit clearance and ATC never says &#x27;cleared&#x27; for taxi. Read back the runway assignment, any clearance to enter a runway, and any hold-short or line-up-and-wait instruction. Ask for progressive taxi if unsure. A taxi clearance does not relieve you of avoiding other aircraft.	far-aim ppl::pre-solo cite::4-3-18

64186304f1b370ef	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	AIM 4-4-6 — Special VFR Clearances	Special VFR: in a Class B, C, D or E surface area with weather below VFR you can ask for a clearance to enter, leave or operate there, traffic permitting. You must stay clear of clouds with at least 1 statute mile flight visibility (and 1 mile ground visibility to take off or land); helicopters are exempt from the mile. Ask the tower, or in a Class E surface area the nearest tower, FSS or Center. No altitude is assigned because you must stay clear of clouds, and you are responsible for terrain and obstacles. Some Class B and C areas prohibit fixed-wing SVFR, and at night fixed-wing SVFR requires an instrument-rated pilot and an IFR-equipped aircraft.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::4-4-6

Navigation	AIM 4-5-7 — Automatic Dependent Surveillance-Broadcast (ADS-B) Services	ADS-B basics: the aircraft broadcasts its GPS position about once a second on 978 MHz (UAT) or 1090 MHz (1090ES); ADS-B Out is required in the § 91.225 airspace and above 18,000 ft it must be 1090ES. Unequipped aircraft may only enter ADS-B airspace with a pre-flight deviation, and if ATC issues a clearance into it you must say you are unequipped. ADS-B In traffic is for visual acquisition only, never for avoidance maneuvers.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::4-5-7

c935682dec4c82b2	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::IX. Emergency Operations	AIM 6-2-4 — Emergency Locator Transmitter (ELT)	Emergency locator transmitters are required on most GA airplanes (§ 91.207). Older units transmit on 121.5 and 243.0 MHz, which satellites stopped monitoring in 2009; 406 MHz units are digital, registered with NOAA, and detected almost instantly. Armed ELTs activate on crash forces and run at least 48 hours. Test analog units only during the first 5 minutes after the hour, for no more than three sweeps; 406 units per the manufacturer; never airborne. Monitor 121.5 before shutdown to catch an accidental activation.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::6-2-4

Listen on 121.5 before engine shutdown to catch an inadvertent activation.<br><br>AIM 6-2-4 — Emergency Locator Transmitter (ELT); § 91.207 — Emergency locator transmitters	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::6-2-4

Emergency Operations	AIM 6-3-3 — Ditching Procedures	Ditching: success depends on sea and wind first, aircraft type second, pilot skill third. Read the swell, land parallel to the primary swell on its crest or back side (never into the face), accept more crosswind to avoid it, judge wind from the streaks on the water, and touch down at the lowest safe speed with the nose up; with no power, carry extra speed to the flare.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::6-3-3

Preflight Preparation	AIM 7-1-3 — Use of Aviation Weather Products	Weather information comes in three types: observations (raw sensor data), analysis (interpreted observations) and forecasts. 8dd370b4721e0d67	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	AIM 7-1-6 — Inflight Aviation Weather Advisories	In-flight advisories. SIGMETs (non-convective) cover severe icing, severe or extreme turbulence or clear air turbulence, dust or sand storms below 3 miles, and volcanic ash; valid 4 hours (6 for tropical cyclones and ash). Convective SIGMETs cover severe thunderstorms (winds 50 knots or more, hail 3/4 inch or more, tornadoes), embedded storms, lines, and heavy precipitation over 40 percent of at least 3,000 square miles; any convective SIGMET implies severe turbulence, severe icing and low-level wind shear; issued hourly at H+55 for the western, central and eastern U.S. AIRMETs (Sierra for IFR and mountain obscuration, Tango for turbulence, surface winds and wind shear, Zulu for icing and freezing level) are lower-intensity, scheduled every 6 hours. Center Weather Advisories are the ARTCC&#x27;s short-term product.	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-1-6
b0904e31afefcde3	Basic	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	What is the difference between an AIRMET and a SIGMET? fd8798594c1b3b0c	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	AIM 7-1-19 — PIREPs Relating to Airframe Icing	Airframe icing: ice cuts thrust and lift, adds drag and weight, and raises stall speed; a half inch can cut lift by half. Expect icing in visible precipitation between +2 and -10 degrees C; if it starts, leave the precipitation or find warmer air. Intensities: trace, light, moderate and severe, defined by accretion rate and how the deice systems cope. Rime ice is rough and opaque from small droplets freezing instantly; clear ice is glossy from large droplets freezing slowly.	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-1-19
c62a795e37de5e2f	Basic	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	What conditions produce airframe ice?	Visible moisture (cloud or precipitation) with the temperature between about +2 and -10 degrees C; freezing rain and drizzle are the worst because their large droplets form clear ice.<br><br>AIM 7-1-19 — PIREPs Relating to Airframe Icing; AIM 7-1-20 — Definitions of Inflight Icing Terms	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-1-19
8c95da7185bbe3d4	Cloze	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	Airframe icing: ice cuts thrust and lift, adds drag and weight, and raises stall speed; a half inch can cut lift by half. Expect icing in visible precipitation between +{{c1::2}} and -10 degrees C; if it starts, leave the precipitation or find warmer air. Intensities: trace, light, moderate and severe, defined by accretion rate and how the deice systems cope. d6f575b43a8c4b51	Cloze	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	Airframe icing: ice cuts thrust and lift, adds drag and weight, and raises stall speed; a half inch can cut lift by half. Expect icing in visible precipitation between +2 and -10 degrees C; if it starts, leave the precipitation or find warmer air. Intensities: trace, light, moderate and severe, defined by accretion rate and how the deice systems cope. {{c1::Rime ice}} is rough and opaque from small droplets freezing instantly; clear ice is glossy from large droplets freezing slowly.	“Rime ice. Rough, milky, opaque ice formed by the instantaneous freezing of small supercooled water droplets.” (AIM 7-1-19 — PIREPs Relating to Airframe Icing)	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-1-19

06814f23449e9270	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	AIM 7-1-21 — PIREPs Relating to Turbulence	Turbulence reporting: light (slight, erratic changes; slight strain on belts), moderate (definite changes, aircraft in positive control; definite strains, objects dislodged), severe (large abrupt changes, momentarily out of control; occupants forced violently against belts) and extreme (practically impossible to control, possible structural damage). &#x27;Chop&#x27; is the rhythmic bumpiness without attitude change. Occasional is less than a third of the time, intermittent a third to two thirds, continuous more. Report location, time, intensity, in or near clouds, altitude, aircraft type and duration.	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-1-21
195ba23e8a6ac4b8	Basic	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	What is the difference between moderate turbulence and moderate chop?	Moderate turbulence changes altitude or attitude and airspeed while the aircraft stays in positive control; moderate chop is rapid rhythmic jolts without appreciable altitude or attitude change.<br><br>AIM 7-1-21 — PIREPs Relating to Turbulence	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-1-21

ba4728b0888a04e9	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	AIM 7-1-24 — Microbursts	Microbursts are intense small-scale downdrafts (up to 6,000 fpm) that spread out on reaching the ground, producing horizontal winds of 45 knots and a 90-knot headwind-to-tailwind shear across a diameter of under a mile (outflow about 2.5 miles). They intensify for about 5 minutes after touching down and seldom last more than 15. They occur in heavy rain and in benign-looking virga, where a ring of blowing dust may be the only clue. Airports with LLWAS, TDWR or WSP issue alerts such as &#x27;microburst alert, 35 knot loss 2 mile final&#x27;.	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-1-24
47a9ce179b0f82ae	Basic	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	What happens to an airplane flying through a microburst on final?	First a headwind and performance gain, then a downdraft and tailwind with a large performance loss, all within a mile or two and a few hundred feet of the ground; the AIM&#x27;s answer is not to be there, and to go around at the first sign.<br><br>AIM 7-1-24 — Microbursts; AIM 4-3-7 — Low Level Wind Shear/Microburst Detection Systems	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-1-24

64db0f79db011f89	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	AIM 7-1-26 — Thunderstorms	Thunderstorms carry turbulence, hail, icing, lightning and up- and downdrafts, and the visible cloud is only part of the system: severe turbulence extends up to 20 miles from a severe storm and about 10 from a less severe one. Radar shows precipitation, not turbulence. Lightning strikes are most likely between -5 and +5 degrees C, even in clear air near the storm. A METAR has no &#x27;severe&#x27; flag, but 50-knot winds or 3/4-inch hail in the report mean a severe storm.	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-1-26

6a088ca719b2fc4b	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	AIM 7-1-27 — Thunderstorm Flying	Thunderstorm do&#x27;s and don&#x27;ts: never take off or land facing an approaching storm, never fly under one or under its anvil, never trust its appearance, never assume ATC will vector you around it, and never use data-linked NEXRAD (15 to 20 minutes old) for tactical maneuvering. Do avoid severe storms by 20 miles, circumnavigate an area with 6/10 coverage, treat tops of 35,000 ft or more as extremely hazardous, give a PIREP, and divert and wait it out if you cannot get around. If penetration is unavoidable: belts tight, hold course and a constant attitude, pick an altitude below the freezing level or above -15 C, pitot heat on, penetration speed set, lights up, autopilot altitude and speed hold off, and never turn back once inside.	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-1-27

05fdfd5c395828e3	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	AIM 7-2-3 — Altimeter Errors	Altimeter procedures: on the ground, a reading 75 ft or more off field elevation makes the altimeter questionable. Flying from high pressure into low without resetting puts you closer to the ground than indicated, about 1,000 ft per inch of error. Below 18,000 ft set a station within 100 nm; above 31.00 inches a NOTAM has everyone set 31.00 en route, with no extra restrictions on VFR aircraft; at 18,000 ft and above set 29.92, and the lowest usable flight level rises as pressure falls (FL180 at 29.92 or higher, FL190 down to 28.92, FL200 below that).	far-aim ppl::solo-xc cite::7-2-3

Preflight Procedures	AIM 7-4-5 — Operations Problem Areas	Where wake bites: below and behind the generating aircraft, especially at low altitude. Be alert in calm wind when vortices linger in the touchdown zone, drift from a nearby runway, sink into a crossing runway&#x27;s path, into the traffic pattern, or into the VFR hemispheric altitude 500 ft below an airliner; a visual approach behind landing traffic is a common scenario. 3d0a31480db62312	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::II. Preflight Procedures	AIM 7-4-6 — Vortex Avoidance Procedures	The avoidance recipes. Landing behind a larger aircraft on the same runway: stay at or above its approach path and land beyond its touchdown point; with a parallel runway closer than 2,500 ft, expect drift; on a crossing runway, cross above its path. Landing behind a departing large aircraft: land well before its rotation point. Departing behind one: rotate before its rotation point, climb above its path and turn away upwind. Be wary after a large aircraft&#x27;s low approach or touch-and-go, and en route stay out from below and behind, moving laterally upwind.	far-aim ppl::pre-solo cite::7-4-6

Preflight Preparation	AIM 7-6-7 — Mountain Flying	Mountain flying: file a flight plan and route over populated areas and known passes with altitude to glide clear; do not fly a light aircraft when winds aloft exceed 35 mph; expect downdrafts of 1,500 to 2,000 fpm on the lee side and add a thousand feet approaching from downwind; cross ridges at a 45-degree angle so you can turn away. Preflight Preparation	AIM 8-1-1 — Fitness For Flight	Fitness for flight: a medical certificate, and the § 61.53 rule that a known disqualifying condition grounds you even with one. cd059c3a662885d5	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	AIM 8-1-2 — Effects of Altitude	Effects of altitude. Hypoxia: night vision degrades from 5,000 ft; significant effects usually start above 12,000 ft (impaired judgment, memory, coordination, euphoria); above 15,000 ft tunnel vision and cyanosis; the ability to act is lost in 20 to 30 minutes at 18,000 ft and 5 to 12 minutes at 20,000. Smoking, carbon monoxide, anemia and some medications lower the threshold. Use oxygen above 10,000 ft by day and 5,000 ft at night. Ear and sinus blocks come from flying with a cold and are not fixed by sprays. After scuba diving wait 12 hours (24 if a decompression stop was needed) before flying up to 8,000 ft and 24 hours before flying higher.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::8-1-2

b197592c43419baa	Basic (and reversed card)	Private Pilot::I. Preflight Preparation	AIM 8-1-3 — Hyperventilation in Flight	Hyperventilation, rapid deep breathing under stress, blows off carbon dioxide and causes lightheadedness, tingling and a feeling of suffocation that can spiral. It clears in minutes when breathing is consciously slowed, or by breathing into a paper bag. Because early symptoms mimic hypoxia and both can occur together, if you are on oxygen go to 100 percent first and check the system before working on your breathing.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::8-1-3

Preflight Preparation	AIM 8-1-5 — Illusions in Flight	Illusions leading to spatial disorientation: the leans, Coriolis, graveyard spin and spiral, somatogravic (acceleration feels like nose-up), inversion, elevator, false horizon and autokinesis; they are prevented only by reference to fixed ground points or the instruments. Illusions leading to landing errors: a narrow or upsloping runway, featureless terrain and rain or haze all make you feel high and fly a low approach; a wide or downsloping runway does the opposite; fog feels like pitching up; bright lights make the runway seem closer.	far-aim ppl::checkride cite::8-1-5

Night Operations	AIM 8-1-8 — Judgment Aspects of Collision Avoidance	Collision-avoidance judgment: use the horizon to judge relative altitude (above the horizon means higher), know the right-of-way rules so you can act, expect the other pilot to maneuver too, and treat any target with no relative motion that grows in size as a collision course.
